Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yeah, it’s nice to meet you too. Well, as for what brings me in... it's kind of a long story, but I guess it boils down to the fact that I feel like I’m always moving from one thing to the next. I just... I really can’t just idle, you know? Like, sitting still and doing nothing, it just doesn't work for me. Even on my days off, I'm always finding a project, going from the garage to the yard to the kitchen, just constantly on the go. 

But at work—I'm a customer service rep—I used to be able to manage it fine. I’d pace around with my wireless headset on while talking to people, or I'd constantly be shifting things around my cubicle, getting up to check the printer, that sort of thing. But recently, management changed everything. We've got this entirely new work setup now, more of an open plan, new desks, the whole nine yards. And honestly, it’s made all this constant moving around a lot harder to hide. Everyone can see me now, and my supervisor has started making comments about how I can't just sit at my station and stay put like everybody else. So, it's really starting to become an issue for me there, and I figured I'd better come talk to someone about it.

I don't really get these sudden spikes where out of nowhere I'm suddenly bouncing off the walls differently than I usually do, you know? It's more of a constant, steady hum that’s just always there. 

Like, take last weekend for example. I woke up at my usual time, maybe 6 AM, and I immediately started sorting through these old boxes of hardware I've had sitting in the corner of the garage since we moved in back in '98. And I just kept going, moved on to weeding the garden, then fixing a squeaky hinge on the back door. But I didn't feel... I don't know, euphoric or anything, or like I had some sudden burst of superpowers. I just had to be doing something. My wife always tells me I never stop, but she knows that's just how I've been since we met back in high school. 

So, no, I wouldn't say I have periods where I feel way more up than my usual self, because my usual self is already the guy pacing around the cubicle and reorganizing the tool bench. It's just my everyday normal, not some special phase or anything like that.

Oh, absolutely. I mean, if you ask my mother, she'd probably talk your ear off about it for hours. It’s funny you ask about before I was 12, because honestly, looking back, this whole thing has been around long, long before this recent job change ever became an issue. 

I remember back in grade school—this would have been, gosh, the early seventies—my report cards always had the exact same comments on them. Every single year, without fail. The teachers would write these little notes at the bottom, and they always said things like, you know, that I was a bright kid, but I was just so careless with my work. Like I’d rush through a math worksheet just so I could get up and go sharpen my pencil or look out the window. 

And the talking! Oh man, I was always getting in trouble for talking out of turn. I just couldn't seem to hold a thought in my head without blurting it out right then and there. I remember Mrs. Gable in fourth grade, she actually moved my desk right next to hers because I just wouldn't stop chatting with the kids around me. She was constantly having to remind me to stay with my work, to sit down, to face forward. 

So yeah, it wasn't just a phase or something that started when I got this customer service job. It’s like I was just born with this engine running a little too hot, and it’s been that way since I was a little kid trying to make it through spelling tests without jumping out of my seat.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, boy. Yeah. That happens... well, it happens a lot more than I'd like to admit, to be perfectly honest with you. It’s incredibly frustrating because, like I said, I’ve been doing this kind of work for a long time, so I know the system inside and out. But I just get going too fast. 

You see, when I'm on the phones, I get into this mode where I just want to power through. I’ll be typing away, and I’ll think, "Okay, great, I’ve finished this customer ticket, everything's handled, on to the next." I hit submit and feel pretty good about it. But then, maybe an hour later, or sometimes the next morning when I log in, the whole thing comes back to me. 

And I'll open it up to see what went wrong, and it's always something so silly. I’ll notice that I typed the completely wrong account number—just transposed a couple of digits because my brain was already three steps ahead of my fingers. Or I'll find that I completely skipped a required box. They have this drop-down menu for "call resolution codes" that you're supposed to fill out before you close the window, and I just blow right past it because I'm in such a hurry to move on. Or I'll have missed one little instruction, like the customer specifically asking to be contacted by email instead of a phone call for the follow-up. Just one tiny little detail like that, and it kicks the whole ticket back into my queue to be completely redone. 

It drives me crazy because I *know* better. It’s not that I don't understand how to do the job, it’s just that my mind drifts off to what I'm going to do next, or I just rush through the steps to get it off my screen. And then I end up making twice the work for myself. It really makes me look careless to the new management team, which, you know, with the new open floor plan and everybody already watching me fidget and pace, is really the last thing I need right now. I just can't seem to force myself to slow down and double-check every little box.

She was constantly having to remind me to stay with my work, to sit down, to face forward. \n\nSo yeah, it wasn't just a phase or something that started when I got this customer service job. It’s like I was just born with this engine running a little too hot, and it’s been that way since I was a little kid trying to make it through spelling tests without jumping out of my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes because your mind drifts or you rush through things?\n\nPatient: Oh, boy. Yeah. That happens... well, it happens a lot more than I'd like to admit, to be perfectly honest with you. It’s incredibly frustrating because, like I said, I’ve been doing this kind of work for a long time, so I know the system inside and out. But I just get going too fast. \n\nYou see, when I'm on the phones, I get into this mode where I just want to power through. I’ll be typing away, and I’ll think, \"Okay, great, I’ve finished this customer ticket, everything's handled, on to the next.\" I hit submit and feel pretty good about it. But then, maybe an hour later, or sometimes the next morning when I log in, the whole thing comes back to me. \n\nAnd I'll open it up to see what went wrong, and it's always something so silly. I’ll notice that I typed the completely wrong account number—just transposed a couple of digits because my brain was already three steps ahead of my fingers. Or I'll find that I completely skipped a required box. They have this drop-down menu for \"call resolution codes\" that you're supposed to fill out before you close the window, and I just blow right past it because I'm in such a hurry to move on. Or I'll have missed one little instruction, like the customer specifically asking to be contacted by email instead of a phone call for the follow-up. Just one tiny little detail like that, and it kicks the whole ticket back into my queue to be completely redone. \n\nIt drives me crazy because I *know* better. It’s not that I don't understand how to do the job, it’s just that my mind drifts off to what I'm going to do next, or I just rush through the steps to get it off my screen. And then I end up making twice the work for myself. It really makes me look careless to the new management team, which, you know, with the new open floor plan and everybody already watching me fidget and pace, is really the last thing I need right now. Throwing things away? No, not really. I mean, I'm not one of those hoarders you see on television, if that's what you're asking. My wife keeps a pretty tidy house, and I like it that way. If something's broken or useless, it goes in the trash. 

Now, I do have my boxes of hardware in the garage, like I mentioned earlier, but that's just practical stuff. Nuts, bolts, some old hinges, maybe a couple of spare spark plugs for the lawnmower. You never know when you're going to need a specific size screw on a Sunday afternoon when the hardware store is closed, right? But it's all organized in those little plastic drawer things. I don't just keep junk for the sake of keeping junk. 

I actually kind of like clearing things out sometimes. It gives me something to do. Every spring, I'll go through the hall closet and pull out all the old coats and boots we don't wear anymore and take a big load down to the Goodwill drop-off. It feels good to just get it out of the house and be productive, you know? Just load up the trunk, drive it over, get the receipt, and boom, done. So, no, I wouldn't say I have any trouble letting go of things when their time is up. I'm pretty good at tossing what needs tossing.

It’s funny you ask about before I was 12, because honestly, looking back, this whole thing has been around long, long before this recent job change ever became an issue. \n\nI remember back in grade school—this would have been, gosh, the early seventies—my report cards always had the exact same comments on them. Every single year, without fail. The teachers would write these little notes at the bottom, and they always said things like, you know, that I was a bright kid, but I was just so careless with my work. Like I’d rush through a math worksheet just so I could get up and go sharpen my pencil or look out the window. \n\nAnd the talking! Oh man, I was always getting in trouble for talking out of turn. I just couldn't seem to hold a thought in my head without blurting it out right then and there. I remember Mrs. Gable in fourth grade, she actually moved my desk right next to hers because I just wouldn't stop chatting with the kids around me. She was constantly having to remind me to stay with my work, to sit down, to face forward. \n\nSo yeah, it wasn't just a phase or something that started when I got this customer service job. It’s like I was just born with this engine running a little too hot, and it’s been that way since I was a little kid trying to make it through spelling tests without jumping out of my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes because your mind drifts or you rush through things?\n\nPatient: Oh, boy. Yeah. That happens... well, it happens a lot more than I'd like to admit, to be perfectly honest with you. It’s incredibly frustrating because, like I said, I’ve been doing this kind of work for a long time, so I know the system inside and out. But I just get going too fast. \n\nYou see, when I'm on the phones, I get into this mode where I just want to power through. I’ll be typing away, and I’ll think, \"Okay, great, I’ve finished this customer ticket, everything's handled, on to the next.\" I hit submit and feel pretty good about it. But then, maybe an hour later, or sometimes the next morning when I log in, the whole thing comes back to me. \n\nAnd I'll open it up to see what went wrong, and it's always something so silly. I’ll notice that I typed the completely wrong account number—just transposed a couple of digits because my brain was already three steps ahead of my fingers. Or I'll find that I completely skipped a required box. They have this drop-down menu for \"call resolution codes\" that you're supposed to fill out before you close the window, and I just blow right past it because I'm in such a hurry to move on. Or I'll have missed one little instruction, like the customer specifically asking to be contacted by email instead of a phone call for the follow-up. Just one tiny little detail like that, and it kicks the whole ticket back into my queue to be completely redone. \n\nIt drives me crazy because I *know* better. It’s not that I don't understand how to do the job, it’s just that my mind drifts off to what I'm going to do next, or I just rush through the steps to get it off my screen. And then I end up making twice the work for myself. It really makes me look careless to the new management team, which, you know, with the new open floor plan and everybody already watching me fidget and pace, is really the last thing I need right now. I just can't seem to force myself to slow down and double-check every little box.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a hard time throwing things away, even when they don’t seem useful anymore?\n\nPatient: Throwing things away? No, not really. I mean, I'm not one of those hoarders you see on television, if that's what you're asking. My wife keeps a pretty tidy house, and I like it that way. If something's broken or useless, it goes in the trash. \n\nNow, I do have my boxes of hardware in the garage, like I mentioned earlier, but that's just practical stuff. Nuts, bolts, some old hinges, maybe a couple of spare spark plugs for the lawnmower. You never know when you're going to need a specific size screw on a Sunday afternoon when the hardware store is closed, right? But it's all organized in those little plastic drawer things. I don't just keep junk for the sake of keeping junk. \n\nI actually kind of like clearing things out sometimes. It gives me something to do. Every spring, I'll go through the hall closet and pull out all the old coats and boots we don't wear anymore and take a big load down to the Goodwill drop-off. It feels good to just get it out of the house and be productive, you know? Just load up the trunk, drive it over, get the receipt, and boom, done. So, no, I wouldn't say I have any trouble letting go of things when their time is up. I'm pretty good at tossing what needs tossing.\n\n[Last Question]\nAs a child or teenager, did you often get into fights or break rules on purpose?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / customer_service_representative / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, gosh no. No, no, no. I wasn't a bad kid, if that's what you're getting at. I mean, you have to understand, I was certainly a handful—like I was saying earlier about Mrs. Gable's class and all the talking and the fidgeting—but I was never malicious about it. I never wanted to hurt anybody or break the rules just for the sake of breaking them. 

My dad, well, he was a pretty strict guy. He worked at the local plant for thirty-something years, and he absolutely would not have stood for any of that kind of nonsense. If I had ever gotten into a real fistfight at school, or if I'd been caught stealing or vandalizing something, oh boy, I would have been in a hundred times worse trouble at home than I ever could have been with the principal. He didn't play around with that sort of disrespect.

Sure, I got sent out into the hall a lot. I spent plenty of time sitting on that little wooden bench outside the main office because I couldn't keep my mouth shut during a lesson, or because I was goofing around in the back of the room trying to make the other kids laugh when I was supposed to be reading. But it was always just clowning around, you know? Just having too much energy and not knowing where to put it. I wasn't out there picking on other kids or looking to start a brawl behind the bleachers after school. I actually got along pretty well with most everybody! I just wanted to be part of whatever was going on, and sometimes I just didn't have the brakes to stop myself from making a joke. So, no, I definitely wasn't a rule-breaker in that rebellious, angry sort of way. Just... easily distracted and maybe a bit too loud for my own good.

Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

And honestly, it’s made all this constant moving around a lot harder to hide. Every single year, without fail. The teachers would write these little notes at the bottom, and they always said things like, you know, that I was a bright kid, but I was just so careless with my work. Like I’d rush through a math worksheet just so I could get up and go sharpen my pencil or look out the window. \n\nAnd the talking! Oh man, I was always getting in trouble for talking out of turn. I just couldn't seem to hold a thought in my head without blurting it out right then and there. I remember Mrs. Gable in fourth grade, she actually moved my desk right next to hers because I just wouldn't stop chatting with the kids around me. She was constantly having to remind me to stay with my work, to sit down, to face forward. \n\nSo yeah, it wasn't just a phase or something that started when I got this customer service job. It’s like I was just born with this engine running a little too hot, and it’s been that way since I was a little kid trying to make it through spelling tests without jumping out of my seat.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
